===PAGE 2=== (ocr, 25 lines)
   1|                                                                 IN MEMORIAM
   2|                                                     Richard J. Bishop was born in Fitchburg, graduated Lunenburg High School,
   3|                                                     attended Fitchburg State College, graduating in 1956 and receiving his Master's
   4|                                                     degree in 1962. He taught sixth grade in 1956 only to be drafted into the U.S. Army
   5|                                                     serving from1957 until 1959. He returned to teaching in Lunenburg and led many
   6|                                                     of his students on new adventures when he instituted the Hiking Club, Biking Club
   7|                                                     and Skiing Club. Dick's final class was in in 1993 when he retired after 36 years of
   8|                                                      dedicated services. Dick was a true "Lunenburger" in every sense of the word
   9|                             serving as Town Moderator for 22 years as well as being involved in Little League, Biddy Basketball
  10|                             and the Community Council. Dick served 50 years in Boy Scouts rising to the rank of Eagle Scout
  11|                             and also enjoyed 50 years in the Lunenburg Grange. Richard received the Joel Miller Outstanding
  12|                             Educator Award from Fitchburg State College, the district Award of Merit from the Boy Scouts of
  13|                             America. In 1966 he was recognized as one of the Ten Outstanding Young Men of the Year in the
  14|                             greater Worcester Area.
  15|                                                     Claire Marie (Cosgrove) Ebersole was born in Malden, Massachusetts and was a
  16|                                                     resident of Lunenburg for 57 years. Mrs. Ebersole was a Librarian at Memorial
  17|                                                     Middle School in Fitchburg MA from its opening in1968 to 1984. She was an
  18|                                                      English Teacher in Vermont in the early 1940's. She graduated from Milton High
  19|                                                      School in 1937, Regis College in 1941 and Boston University in 1942 with a
  20|                                                     Master's Degree in English. She was a very active member of the Lunenburg
  21|                             Historical Society, a Red Cross volunteer, a Lunenburg Library Trustee and a Cub Scout Den
  22|                              mother. She served on the high school curriculum advisory committee. Over the years she
  23|                             volunteered countless hours at the Library and Historical Society. She was an avid Red Sox fan.
  24|                              She was a Veteran of World War Il, serving as a Lieutenant, jg in the U.S. Navy WAVES from 1943 to
  25|                              1945 and was awarded the American Campaign Medal and World War I Victory Medal.

===PAGE 9=== (ocr, 44 lines)
   1|                                                                            REPORT OF THE TOWN MANAGER
   2|                                    The Town continued to make important progress in 2013 in many areas, including:                   School Building; Regional
   3|                                    Service Delivery; Municipal Electric Aggregation; and Building Reuse.                In 2013, we renewed our consultant
   4|                                    contract for our Municipal Electric Aggregation Plan with Colonial Power Group. Under the Aggregation Plan,
   5|                                    the Town, on behalf of rate payers, bids electric supply service with various suppliers. If the Town can secure
   6|                                    a better rate that what Unitil can offer as its default rate, then the Town will make that rate available. If not,
   7|                                    then Unitil's default rate stands. Since the inception of the Aggregation Plan in January 2012, the Town has
   8|                                    almost always been able to offer a more competitive rate. In total, Lunenburg residents and business
   9|                                    collectively have saved $183,301.72 under the Aggregation Plan!
  10|                                    In 2013, the School Building Committee continued its work on the Feasibility Study & Preliminary Design
  11|                                    Report, with the end goal being to bring to Town Meeting a recommendation on either building a new or
  12|                                   renovating an existing school building. Please review the Superintendent of School's report for additional
  13|                                    information.
  14|                                    In June 2013, the Town's E-911 Service was transitioned over to Nashoba Valley Regional Dispatch District
  15|                                    (NVRDD), a newly created district which provides E-911 Service to the towns of Harvard, Lancaster,
  16|                                    Lunenburg and to Devens. Lunenburg is the largest community in the district and it was the first town to
  17|                                    transition service. The decision to transition to the district was made after years of study and review of two
  18|                                    separate regional options. New state requirement to provide Emergency Medical Dispatch in addition to the
  19|                                   increasing number of calls for service would have necessitated an increase in staffing levels, resulting in a
  20|                                    higher cost to the Lunenburg taxpayer. Ultimately, it was determined that by opting for a regional service, we
  21|                                    could meet the new state requirements within our existing budget. From an end-user point of view, there has
  22|                                    been no change in service levels. In FY2015, the District anticipates expanding to include an additional town
  23|                                    which will reduce the annual assessment to each of the existing district members.
  24|                                    In October 2013, the Town received word that its Bond Rating had been increased from AA- to AAt, a
  25|                                    significant jump of two grades. Since 2009, the Town's bond rating has increased four grades from A to AA+.
  26|                                   The increase was made in recognition of the Town's overall improved financial condition, adherence to
  27|                                   adopted financial policies, and fiscal prudence. The increase in Bond Rating comes at a great time and will
  28|                                    In November 2013, the Board of Selectmen accepted a $25,000 grant from the Stanton Foundation which will
  29|                                   enable the Lunenburg Police Department to implement a K-9 Program. The grant will cover the cost of
  30|                                   purchasing, training and caring for the dog for the first 3 years of the program. The K-9 Unit will be used in
  31|                                   narcotics detection, assisting in locating missing persons, and in following suspects involved in crime.
  32|                                   In 2013, the Board of Selectmen established a Building Reuse Committee, specifically tasking it with the
  33|                                   following: identify space requirements for potential combined municipal operations; identify costs to convert
  34|                                   municipal space; identify 5 and 10 year capital costs to retain existing buildings including, Town Hall, Ritter
  35|                                   Memorial and Eagle House; identify appropriate options for sale or lease of facilities; and identify potential
  36|                                   reuse or market for any buildings deemed surplus as a result of consolidation. The Committee made its report
  37|                                   of findings to the Board in December. As a result, additional tasks were identified, including: communicating
  38|                                   findings and recommendations so they can be considered during School Building discussion; develop a "go
  39|                                                plan for the marketable properties; create a Request for Proposals Template after discussion with
  40|                                   stakeholders; develop consolidation plans for municipal functions and a renovations plan for Town Hall; and
  41|                                   make bottom-line cost and investment decisions and review sale and revenue projections. The Committee will
  42|                                   continue its work in 2014.
  43|                                   I would be remiss if I did not take this opportunity to thank all of the people in this town who give of their time
  44|                                   and many talents to make Lunenburg a better place. Thank you all for your dedicated service.

   6|                                                                                     PLANNING BOARD
   7|                  The Planning Board welcomed new members Damon McQuaid and Matthew Allison. Emerick Bakaysa stepped down as Chair, and
   8|                   Joanna Bilotta-Simeone was voted in as new Chair.
   9|                  The number of ANR plans (subdivision of lots for separate building lots) submitted for 2013 was three (3).
  10|                  The major large project, Emerald Place at Lake Whalom, continued construction according to the approved plan with three different
  11|                  housing styles. They are constructed villas with two (2) units (single floor design) per structure. The developer's plans are to construct
  12|                  to completion. The units are occupied as each unit or set of units are completed, contracted under "lease to own" agreements.
  13|                  Tri Town Landing completed construction on Building Three (3). The 40R project Buildings 1, 2, and 3 are occupied as rentals and all
  14|                  ninety-nine (99) units have been rented. The Town has received compliments on the construction and management of this project.
  15|                  Stone Farm, Massachusetts Avenue is still being constructed to completion. Highfield Village, Northfield Road is still in review. Several
  16|                  extensions have been given.           The project entitled Whitetail Crossing has submitted its "As Built" and plans on requesting Town
  17|                  acceptance of the roadway interior. Sequoia Drive and Whites Woods Phase Ill are building upon request.
  18|                  The Open Space Plan, under the auspices of the Planning Board and Conservation Commission, was reported previously as approved by
  19|                  the State. The Ad Hoc Committee for that Plan continues to work, as recommended, to address implementation. At Annual Town Meeting
  20|                  2013, the citizens voted to approve the establishment of an Agricultural Commission to represent, preserve and enhance farming in the
  21|                  community. The Commission is also working on the creation of a "Right to Farm" bylaw. This work continued in 2013 and will involve the
  22|                  farming community. Many farmers have shown interest. They are working with a State Consultant.
  23|                  Work began in 2012 to update the Master Plan and Zoning Bylaw. In 2013, the Board crafted a Citizen Survey for the Lunenburg Master
  24|                  Plan. The input that is received in these completed surveys will be used to update the Town's Master Plan to reflect the values of the
  25|                  citizens. Approximately 270 surveys were completed.
  26|                  In addition, the Planning Board continues to work with MRPC on a DLTA (District Local Technical Assistance) Grant for services related to
  27|                  investigating/researching and working to adopt a mixed use Village District Bylaw in the downtown area that could promote economic
  28|                  development while retaining small town community character.
  29|                  Chair Joanna Bilotta-Simeone is the Board's representative to the Montachusetts Regional Planning Commission. Matthew Allison is the
  30|                 representative to the Montachusett Joint Transportation Commission. Nathan Lockwood is the representative to the School Building
  31|                  Committee and Damon McQuaid is the representative to the Building Reuse Committee.
  32|                  The Planning Board continues to work toward the Goals as identified in the Master Plan for the betterment of the Town.
  33|                  Joanna L. Bilotta-Simeone, Chair                     Matthew Allison, Member
  34|                  Nathan J. Lockwood, Vice Chair                       Marion M. Benson, Planning Director

  37|                                                                            LUNENBURG PUBLIC LIBRARY
  38|                 The Town of Lunenburg met the standards set by The State Board of Library Commissioners and the Lunenburg Public Library was duly
  39|                 and fully certified last year. This entitled us to $11,709 in State Aid Funding. These monies are primarily used for technology such as the
  40|                 Integrated Library System, our membership in the library network - CWMARS (which enables our residents to borrow from over 300 other
  41|                 libraries), and Overdrive which provides free access to E-books.
  42|                                                                                                •10

  36|                                                                            GREEN COMMUNITY TASK FORCE                                                                                                 pl
  37|                                                                                                                                                                                                       SE
  38|                 This has been a very busy year for the Green Community Task Force. We continue to focus our efforts on providing information to Town
  39|                 officials and residents with respect to Green Energy alternatives. The largest undertaking this year was the Town passing the stretch
  40|                 code and therefore paving the path for Green Community Status in Lunenburg. Our application was submitted in October and on
  41|                 December 18th we received notification that Lunenburg has been accepted as a Green Community with a Grant award of $157,225.
  42|                 The Task Force met on eight occasions throughout the year and dealt with multiple Green topics ranging from solar electric and hot water
  43|                 generation, wind turbine energy, recycling opportunities, delivering of information and resources as well as the Green Communities Act.
  44|                                                                                                    11

===PAGE 15=== (ocr, 43 lines)
   1|                    We are now evaluating the town buildings and             generating the Grant Application for the funds awarded to us. The projects are being
   2|                    ranked by several criteria including; immediate energy conservation, long term use of building, projects that can use outside funding and
   3|                    impact on the daily use of the building.
   4|                    The Task force looks forward to working closer with the other departments in town to develop and implement other energy saving
   5|                    measures throughout the town of Lunenburg.
   6|                    The Task Force generally meets during the first week of each month at 7:00 PM at the Ritter Memorial Building and on an as-needed basis
   7|                    when required.

  14|                                                                                      BOARD OF HEALTH
  15|                    The Board of Health office is located in the old Ritter Library Building on the corner of Mass Avenue and Leominster Road. Our office is
  16|                    on the upper level at the end of the hall on the right. We can be reached at 978-345-4146, extension 430.
  17|                    The Board of Health, with the assistance of its Agent, the Nashoba Associated Boards of Health, continued its mission to maintain its
  18|                    focus on public health and safety issues during 2013, reviewing Title V inspection reports, building permits, septic and well permits,
  19|                    nuisance investigations, housing inspections, complaints and general public health related issues.
  20|                    Homeland Security planning is ongoing. Meetings continue between our agents, other town departments and regional entities to develop
  21|                    a plan for not only Lunenburg but with our partner towns in our region.
  22|                    This year, we processed applications for Septic Permit Lot Tests, permits for new construction, new wells, repairs or upgrades to septic
  23|                    systems and well permits. The Board continues its vigilance with regard to septic systems, wells, food service inspections, housing and
  24|                    nuisance complaints and its general mandate to protect the environment and groundwaters of the Commonwealth.
  25|                   The Board of Health provides numerous other services in cooperation with our Health Agents, The Nashoba Associated Boards of Health.
  26|                   In addition to our environmental services, numerous clinic are provided throughout the year to our residents. These clinics provide
  27|                   inoculations, blood pressure screenings, well oldster clinics and dental screening. Fluoride treatment and dental checks of our
  28|                   youngsters in school system are provided by the Nashoba dental staff. With our Nahoba Nursing and Hospice partners we provide in-
  29|                   home visits to all residents recovering from illness and hospice care and counseling to those in needs of this service.

  31|                   efforts on behalf of you, the Citizens of Lunenburg. If you have something that you think we can help you with, we are at your service.

  25|                                                                               HISTORICAL COMMISSION
  26|                  The Historical Commission consists of five appointed volunteers who meet once a month from September to June. The work of the
  27|                 Historical Commission is preservation and appreciation of all things historic in Town of Lunenburg. The Historical Commission has
  28|                 through the past year been an advisor on the Building Reuse Committee with members Patrick Slattery and Damon McQuaid serving as
  29|                 our representatives. We have continued to have our members write articles for The Lunenburg Ledger's "Historically Speaking" column.
  30|                 We are nearing completion of the photo survey project for the Massachusetts Historical Commission through the monumental efforts of
  31|                 member, Damon McQuaid. We continue to hold hearings and enact the Demolition Delay By-Law of the Town of Lunenburg for the
  32|                 destruction of Historic properties. This past year we saw the loss of the Padula house on Leominster/Shirley Road.
  33|                 We express our gratitude to member Patrick Slattery for his 5 years of recent service to our Commission. He has been involved in various
  34|                 other Commissions and boards in Town for over 20 years. We are in need of another member to complete our five persons Commission;
  35|                please submit your interest through a Talent Bank Form in the Town Hall. Current members of the Historical Commission: Amory Phelps, Rebecca Lantry, Damon McQuaid, Richard McGrath.
  36|                                                                           TECHNOLOGY DEPARTMENET
  37|                The primary mission of the Information Technology Department is to increase efficiency through the use of technology. The IT
  38|                Department enables all other town departments and schools to communicate and collaborate, manage daily tasks, organize, store, and
  39|                share information, and generally conduct business more effectively. We also provide the necessary infrastructure, equipment, software,
  40|                and expertise to comply with current regulations and methodology when interacting with outside agencies and organizations.
  41|                Our staff has remained the same this year, but our network administrator Josh Branham has taken on a full time position following
  42|                graduating from Fitchburg State.
  43|                                                                                                15

===PAGE 19=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|               Steve Malandrinos - Information Technology Director
   2|                      Hired in December, 2012
   3|                   • Previously an engineer at Cisco Systems, IT Director for the Town of Belchertown, MA and Belchertown Public Schools
   4|              Josh Branham - Network Administrator
   5|                    • Over six years working for the town and school system
   6|                   • Stepping into a full time role this year having successfully completed his Bachelor's Degree in Computer Information Systems - Congratulations Josh!
   7|              Alex Smith - Systems Administration (part time)
   8|                   • Over three years working for the town and school system
   9|                   • Currently half way through his senior year at Fitchburg State majoring in Computer Science
  10|               Along with serving as helpdesk for all users daily technology issues and the usual maintenance and scheduled replacement of the town'
  11|               and schools' technology assets, the IT Department has been involved with several high impact projects throughout the past fiscal year
  12|              The following examples are among the more noteworthy improvements that IT has implemented.
  13|              We have continued our efforts to virtualize all server hardware via VMware Sphere. Lunenburg High School, Middle School, and Primary
  14|              School, as well as the Public Safety complex servers have all been converted to virtual machines. This conversion provides greatly
  15|              enhanced management and backup, while creating a hardware-independent environment. If a physical server fails, the virtual servers can
  16|              be moved to almost any available hardware, simplifying disaster recovery.
  17|              An enterprise-style Wi-Fi solution has been deployed at the Public Safety Complex. Replacing older, consumer grade equipment has
  18|             given us universal coverage within the complex. The system also offers an open guest network for visitors to access the internet, while
  19|              simultaneously providing a secure connection to internal networks and services for our police officers, fire fighters, EMS personnel and
  20|             administration.
  21|             The technology department also assisted with the implementation of fleet management software for the Department of Public Works.
  22|             Keeping track of use and maintenance schedules is absolutely critical for an organization which relies on a large and varied fleet of
  23|             vehicles to perform the ongoing tasks of keeping Lunenburg's physical infrastructure safe and functional for all town residents and
  24|             visitors.
  25|             We continue to strive to leverage limited resources to improve efficiency wherever possible.                  I am very happy to have stepped into this
  26|             role, and I couldn't be more pleased with the level of skill, hard work and dedication of my staff, Josh and Alex. I know that because of
  27|             them we will continue to improve the technology landscape in Lunenburg while maintaining the positive reputation they helped to garner
  28|             over the past few years.

  35|             Administrative Assistant to the Zoning Board.
  36|             The functions of your Zoning Board of Appeals as required by statures are as follows:
  37|                  1. To hear and decide applications for Variances from Town's Protective By-Laws as authorized in the By-laws.
  38|                  2. To hear and decide applications for Special Permits as authorized in the Town's Protective By-laws.
  39|                 3. To hear and decide appeals in accordance with Chapter 40A, Section 8 of the Massachusetts General Laws, as amended, from
  40|                       any person aggrieved by reason of his or her inability to obtain a permit or enforcement action from the Administrative Officer or
  41|                       Board in the Town, the Regional Planning Agency, abutting City or Town or other Administrative Official.

   7|                                                                       DEPARTMENT OF PUBLIC WORKS
   8|               The Department of Public Works combines the resources of the Highway, Park, Cemetery, Sewer, and Facilities divisions, and Vehicle
   9|               Maintenance services into one department organizing and scheduling personnel and equipment to best serve the residents of the
  10|              community through the effective delivery of an extensive scope services.
  11|              DEPARTMENTAL OBJECTIVES
  12|              "Public Works is either working or on call twenty four hours a day three hundred and sixty five days a year to respond to any weather,
  13|              infrastructure, or facility related emergency that may arise.
  14|              In the upcoming year we will strive to enhance the awareness of the value of Public Works provides to our Town, and ensures others are
  15|              aware of our contribution. This is not for an expectation of praise, but rather to make sure efforts are not forgotten as they merge into the                                    S
  16|              fabric of our everyday service.
  17|              Major events over the past years have highlighted the fact that Public Works has a seat at the table and is a major player in emergency
  18|              management. Town Officials, Police, and Fire acknowledge that Public Works is critical in order to facilitate the required services of
  19|              others. We must continue to build on this awareness.
  20|              This department must also reach out to the public we serve and educate them to the fact that the common services they receive do not
  21|              just happen, but rather are the efforts of a dedicated staff of individuals, service companies, and vendors. Safe roadways, wastewater
  22|              control, solid waste collection and recycling operations, tree removal, storm water management facilities and grounds maintenance,
  23|              winter snow and ice removal, cemeteries and parks are the expected services supporting everyone in the community.                                                                S
  24|              We will work to engage the public in order to set reasonable expectations and gather support for annual budget requests, capital                                                 A
  25|              requests, and specific projects. (Adapted from APWA)
  26|                                                                                                                                                                                               S
  27|              PAVEMENT MANAGEMENT
  28|              We are currently in the process of upgrading our Pavement Management Plan working with a consultant to establish an electronic data
  29|              base and a revised Town-wide pavement defect analysis. An ever increasing backlog of roadway rehabilitation will continue to be                                                  E
  30|              prioritized with established criteria and addressed as funding allows. Road projects typically extend over two construction seasons and
  31|              from one fiscal year to the next. Improvements were completed on Lancaster Ave., Fort Pond Road, a portion of Reservoir Rd., New West                                            S
  32|              Townsend Rd to the Townsend line, a section of Lakefront Ave. beyond the scope of Emerald Place, and Chase Rd. (Rt. 13) from the 2A
  33|              taking to just south of the Northfield Rd. intersection. The scope of the projects included: a leveling course of asphalt followed by a top or                                  al
  34|              finish course, sections of "berm" or curbing, shoulder work, minor drainage improvements and driveway aprons.
  35|                                                                                                                                                                                              TI
  36|              We continue to extend the scope of our pavement preservation methods with the addition of "crack sealing" to the overall program. This                                          ec
  37|              process involves the installation of rubberized asphalt under high pressure to cracks or separations in the pavement. This application can                                      pl
  38|             add approximately 5 years or more to the existing surface. The Pavement Management Plan also identifies specific areas where this                                                A:
  39|              method is the best option. Please understand this method does not take the place of curb to curb paving, and is not applicable to other                                         m
  40|              more serious defects.
  41|              Funding continues to be the major detriment to improving the overall condition of our roadways. The primary source of funding is the                                            W
  42|              Massachusetts Department of Transportation Chapter 90 program. The estimated value of our 90 miles of paved streets is, by industry
  43|              standards, calculated to be 62 million dollars. The General Highway maintenance line within the operational budget has remained
  44|              stagnant for two decades while the overall omnibus budget has nearly tripled to in excess of 30 million dollars.. In order to initiate and                                      36
  45|             sustain a road improvement program a substantial infusion of funds is necessary. A conservative estimate would be approximately 1.2                                              Ir
  46|             million dollars per year total or an additional $800,000.00 to existing allocations. Applying $400,000 a year to a backlog of work that                                          Th
  47|              exceeds 10 million dollars places this department and the Town as a whole in dismal failure. Many roads are compromised to the degree                                           wi
  48|             that only a major reconstruction can bring them to an acceptable quality. Patching, or filling pot holes by DPW staff is in most areas is no                                     ea
  49|             longer a feasible or successful application.
  50|             Without a substantial infusion of funds our roads Town-wide will continue to deteriorate to a point where the safety of our travelling public                                    ap
  51|             will be compromised.

  19|               ENVIRONMENTAL
  20|               Storm Water
  21|               The Environmental Protection Agency has not yet set the requirements for the next phase of storm water regulation and permits. As we
  22|               await the potential of yet another unfunded Federal mandate the Town remains in compliance with current protocol.
  23|               The DPW, as a result of limited funding is forced to become reactionary to roadway drainage issues. A capital program in the 1990's earmarked $20,000.00 annually toward rehabilitation/installation of piping and catch basins. The elimination of this dedicated funding
  24|               placed the financial burden upon the overall General Highway maintenance allocation effectively reducing drainage and roadway repairs.
  25|               As roadways are scheduled for pavement,               drainage is surveyed and necessary repairs are addressed. Little or no preventative
  26|               maintenance, short of catch basin cleaning can be done due to a lack of available resources..
  27|               The DPW continues as the caretaker of the entire sewer system from permitting, inspections, and daily preventative maintenance of Waste Water
  28|               collector lines and pumping stations to billing and documentation. There is but one full time person who diligently remains on call, 24/7 365 for all sewer related emergencies.
  29|               The infamous "Ice Storm of '08" continues to detrimentally affect trees in the Town right-of-way. Previously unnoticed damage together Tree Removal
  30|               with dying limbs creates a backlog of tree elimination and pruning that far exceeds the allotted budget. The department is unable to fulfill each resident request and must prioritize work based upon the perceived degree of danger to residents and property. A more efficient
  31|                approach would involve a survey of all Town owned trees with a generated list of those requiring attention. Until a plan is in place, the DPW will act in a reactionary mode. Al work aside from brush removal, chipping, and the felling of small diameter trees is outsourced due
  32|                to the required use of a bucket truck.
  33|                                                                                               18

===PAGE 22=== (ocr, 42 lines)
   1|               The "capped" landfill off Young's Rd. is in compliance with the Division of Environmental Protection. The site is monitored regularly for excessive methane gas as well as the possibility of groundwater contamination.
   2|               This year the frequency of the testing was reduced through a special permit acquired as a result of consistently favorable results. The
   3|               landfill site including the perimeter fencing is inspected on a regular schedule by DPW personnel.
   4|                Adjacent to the "cap" is a storage area for catch basin materials and street sweepings both determined by the EPA to be hazardous
   5|               waste. We are permitted to store these materials through the procurement of a (BUD) Beneficial Use Determination permit. The DEP requires yearly testing of these materials.
   6|               On weekends during the fall and springtime months DPW personnel oversee and manage the annual composting of brush, leaves and
   7|               other organic debris providing a valuable service to our residents and add a valuable component to our recycling program. There has to
   8|               date been no dedicated funding toward the organization or long range remediation of this location. Once again, dollars will be taken from
   9|               the General Highway line to maintain this location while keeping it environmentally safe.
  10|               Solid Waste
  11|               Public works monitors, orders, stores, and distributes trash bags in order to facilitate the curbside collection of trash and recyclables by
  12|               our current vendor, Casella Inc.
  13|              Lake Shirley Dam
  14|               In the early 1990's, with the structure in need of repair/rebuilding The Lake Shirley Association turned over ownership of the dam to the
  15|               Town. After completion of a total reconstruction, responsibilities for maintenance, monitoring, and record keeping was completed solely
  16|               by a small number of volunteers from the Lake Association. In 2004-2005 custodianship of the dam, compliance with State dam
  17|               regulations, the establishment of an operations manual as well as an emergency action plan was determined to come under the umbrella
  18|               of the Department of Public Works. As with the storm water program and other EPA / DEP mandates compliance with current and future
  19|              regulations is critical. We rely on the expertise and support of a professional consultant to assist with predetermined mandates. The most recent inspection resulted in a positive report.
  20|              Winter Operations
  21|              Snow and ice present a major challenge to our Town. Each Town department and the safety of the general public depend on the ability of
  22|              the DPW to maintain our roadways in a safe condition despite the severity or the duration of snow and ice events. To that end we develop
  23|              a winter operations procedure based upon a well thought out plan that must be flexible due to the unpredictability of each storm. A
  24|              consistently high level of service is expected and must be delivered to every part of town in a timely manner.
  25|              Preparations begin in the fall with the capital plan. Rotation of the fleet in order to reduce breakdowns and minimize repairs is contingent
  26|              upon resident support. Also in the fall, salt and sand are delivered to the Chase Rd. facility as vehicle maintenance staff readies the
  27|              sanders and plows. Over the summer and fall road crews address right-of-way issues, overhanging branches, and pavement defects in order to eliminate obstacles to plowing. Each division within the DPW is involved in winter operations.
  28|              Generally it is not the amount of snow but the duration of the event that determines the overall cost. For example, a fast moving snow
  29|              storm of 6-8 inches will cost significantly less than a long duration freezing rain event even though the accumulation is much less.
  30|              Historically, the department operates in a deficit due to a yearly allocation that is below the cost of an average winter.
  31|             PARKS
  32|             In conjunction with the three elected Park Commissioners DPW staff oversees and maintains all parks and grounds exclusive of school
  33|             property. A full time Park Superintendent, lost through attrition, was replaced with one seasonal employee. Much of the Town wide
  34|             landscape maintenance was outsourced, resulting in a significant savings. The new multi-use track complex remains under the control of
  35|             the Commissioners with maintenance support from DPW personnel.
  36|             CEMETERY
  37|             One Cemetery Division Superintendent, a DPW employee, handles the duties of the North and South cemeteries, maintaining the grounds,
  38|             coordinating burials, and meeting regularly with the three members of the Cemetery Commission. The DPW supplies equipment and support staff for, on average, forty burials a year.
  39|             through these difficult times. As always, 1 am deeply appreciative of the continued patience and support of our residents and fellow Town employees as we persevere
  40|             "As caretakers of the Town's infrastructure our goal is to preserve and enhance the resources of the Town of Lunenburg for future
  41|             generations. To that end we strive to meet or exceed all expectations associated with the challenges of each of the divisions under the umbrella of public works"

===PAGE 23=== (ocr, 49 lines)
   1|                                                                                   SEWER COMMISSION
   2|                  The five member Sewer Commission was originally established at the Special Town Meeting on May 6, 2006. During 2013, Carl Luck and
   3|                  Mike Nault were re-elected for three year terms at the Town Election and the officers remained the same: Carl Luck -Chairman of the
   4|                  Sewer Commission, Jay Simeone- Vice-Chairman and Dave MacDonald -Clerk. The other members are Butch Bilotta and Mike Nault. The
   5|                  Sewer Commission bears the responsibility of overseeing the Town's sewer system, planning for the future, and developing and
   6|                  implementing rules, regulations and policies. The Department of Public Works manages and maintains the operational side of the system.
   7|                  Under the Town's Charter, responsibility for the day-to day operation, care and maintenance of the sanitary sewers are under the
   8|                  supervision of the Town Manager. The Town of Lunenburg does not operate a sewer treatment plant. Approximately 73% of Lunenburg
   9|                  sewer is discharged to the City of Leominster, and the other 27% is discharged to the City of Fitchburg.
  10|                  There are currently 723 properties connected to the Lunenburg system and another 351 that have access to Town Sewer but are not yet
  11|                  connected. During 2013, thirty-six new accounts connected to Town Sewer. The Commission billed out $617,352 in quarterly sewer bills,
  12|                  and had $53,730 of prior year delinquencies added to the owners' property tax bills for non-payment. Eight properties with access to
  13|                  sewer were assessed privilege fees that had not originally been assessed betterments. Privilege fees help to pay down the debt owed by
  14|                 the Town on the original sewer project.
  15|                  The Sewer Commission was very proud to present a balanced budget during the FY14 budget process for the first time since the inception
  16|                 improve the operating efficiency of the system.
  17|                  During 2013, the Sewer Commission solicited Proposals to convert from in-house service of the 10 pumping stations and sewer
  18|                 infrastructure to having an outside contractor perform the service and maintenance. Four companies presented bids. The Sewer
  19|                 Commission selected Small Water Systems Services of Littleton for a one-year contract, with the potential for up to three years, starting in
  20|                 August 2013. Corey Dowd who had served as the Sewer Technician moved over to the Highway division of the DPW. As of the end of
  21|                 2013, the Commission has been very satisfied with the personnel, the work and the arrangement with Small Water Systems Services.
  22|                 The Sewer Commission had several large projects throughout 2013. The largest and most comprehensive was the revision and
  23|                 implementation of the Sewer Use Regulations, which became effective September 2013. With this document, all bylaws, policies, rules and
  24|                 regulations were compiled into one document. One of the new policies developed and utilized this year was the Illegal Connection Policy
  25|                 for use when a property connects to the Town Sewer without obtaining the proper permits. A minimum fee of $5000 will be levied to the
  26|                 property owner, along with back usage charges, if the Commission learns of an illegal connection. For the 90 days prior to its
  27|                 implementation, the Commission offered "amnesty" to anyone who knowingly was connected and was not paying a sewer bill. In
  28|                 Lunenburg, the water and sewer is billed separately, so a resident would know that they are illegally connected if they do not have a
  29|                 septic system and do not receive a quarterly sewer bill. The Commission would encourage any resident in that situation come forward to
  30|                 Another major focus of the Commission was to enforce the Grease Trap Regulation by contacting all Food Service Establishments that
  31|                 have or should have a grease trap or grease interceptor. Annual permit fees were assessed based on the establishment's level of
  32|                 compliance, and the reporting requirements clearly communicated. Lunenburg has 19 grease trap establishments and has received the
  33|                 permit fees from 17 of them by the end of 2013. This regulation will continue to be strongly enforced as Fats, Oils and Grease (FOG) can
  34|                 negatively affect the smooth operation and maintenance costs of the sewer system. While the Grease Trap Regulation focuses on
  35|                 commercial and public disposal, individual home owners can also contribute a significant amount of grease into the system which
  36|                 increases everyone's costs. The Commission understands that education is an important aspect of their role, and will continue to provide
  37|                 information to their customers to keep the sewer system running economically and efficiently by reducing the disposal of fats, oil and
  38|                 grease into the system to avoid blockages. Whether on sewer or septic, grease should not be put down the drain. Another enemy of the
  39|                 sewer system are "disposable wipes" which do not bio-degrade and bring our pumps to a halt. Nothing but organic waste and toilet paper
  40|                 should be put into the sewer system.
  41|                 A major goal of the Commission is to increase Sewer revenues by adding new customers both within the currently sewered areas as well
  42|                 as identified needs areas per the 2010 Town-funded Comprehensive Wastewater Management Plan (CWMP). During 2013, the Commission
  43|                 worked closely with several resident groups interested in sewer extensions to connect to public sewer. One of the major projects for this
  44|                 year was the Commission's work with the group of 7 residents on Lancaster Ave. who worked with the Commission on the installation of
  45|                 a privately-funded Sewer extension from Whiting St., and accepted by the Town on November 7, 2013. A second group came forward with
  46|                 a request for the Commission's assistance to extend the sewer from Prospect Ave. to the Pine Grove Rd. area, as well as the Sunset Ave.
  47|                 area. This last extension would serve 42 parcels and eliminate an environmental concern for Whalom Lake, An application was therefore
  48|                 put forward to the State to apply for low-interest loans from the State Revolving Fund (SRF). Another area of Town that had come forward
  49|                                                                                                20

===PAGE 24=== (ocr, 48 lines)
   1|                   in the past with interest in connecting to sewer (Pratt St. & Rennie St.) was included in the same application to the State. Our proposal
   2|                   attained a sufficient rating to be assured of an SRF loan and the Commission will present the project at the spring Town Meeting to obtain
   3|                   bottom line by providing more revenue to the Sewer Enterprise Fund. approval to move the project forward. These extensions as proposed would have little impact to the system costs, but would improve the
   4|                   The Commission also continued to work diligently with the Lunenburg Board of Selectmen and the City of Fitchburg to complete an
   5|                   updated Inter-Municipal Agreement (IMA). Key sections of the new agreement, which went into effect 12/15/13, include converting the basis by which we pay Fitchburg to the amount of outflow as measured by in-ground flow meters at the Town line. This has the potential
   6|                  to increase the cost for flow to Fitchburg and further drive efforts to reduce unwanted inflow and infiltration (I/l). Another major
   7|                  accomplishment of the new IMA was the virtual elimination of a use "adder" which was a significant portion of the cost. These two issues
   8|                  will hopefully offset each other from a cost point of view and result in a negligible impact to overall system operating costs. The new IMA
   9|                  also includes terms for Lunenburg to pay for its portion of Fitchburg's proposed John Fitch Highway improvement project in order to
  10|                  secure a guarantee of access for future projected needs. This represents the first time Lunenburg has had to pay directly for capacity and
  11|                  will drive the need for a new policy to assure a fair sharing of these costs by new users. Chairman Carl Luck and past Chairman and
  12|                  current Board of Selectmen liaison, Bob Ebersole, worked in a highly cooperative way with Fitchburg officials to get this agreement
  13|                  renewed with terms that are more favorable to the Town of Lunenburg. The Sewer Commission is grateful to them for their hard work and
  14|                  perseverance in getting this important agreement between Fitchburg and Lunenburg finalized. The Commission would like to thank Bob
  15|                  for continuing his affiliation with the Sewer Commission as BOS liaison after stepping off the Commission in 2012. His knowledge and
  16|                  counsel continue to be sought and valued by the Commission.
  17|                 They would also like to thank John Male for his years of dedicated and valuable service as the FinCom liaison to the Commission as he
  18|                 steps off the Finance Committee and they wish him well. At the same time, they welcome Jason Smith as their new liaison. The
  19|                  Commission would also like to thank Jack Rodriquenz, DPW Director, for the seamless and smooth operation of the Sewer division of the
  20|                 DPW and also Jim Breault (DPW-Facilities) who, when called upon, also provides his expertise and service to the Sewer Department. The
  21|                 Commission would like to thank Corey Dowd for his hard work as Sewer Technician over the past years and wish him well as he
  22|                 continues on with the DPW in the Highway Division. Finally, we would like to acknowledge the hard work, initiative and dedication of Barb
  23|                 Lefebvre, Business Manager for the Lunenburg Sewer Commission.

  27|                                                                                    FIRE DEPARTMENT
  28|                   The Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians of the Lunenburg Fire Department are dedicated to providing the highest quality,
  29|                                        most cost effective fire and emergency medical services to the citizens of the town of Lunenburg.
  30|                           In 2013 your Fire Department responded to 1733 calls for service, broken down as follows:
  31|                           Fires & Hazardous Conditions                          412                  Emergency Medical 801
  32|                           Inspections                                           459                  Other                       61
  33|                           Fire Department staffing today is 5 Career Staff and 45 On Call Firefighters and Emergency Medical Technicians. The Station is
  34|                staffed Monday through Friday from 6am-6pm. However, on weeknights and weekends all of our personnel respond from home on an "on
  35|                call" basis. This adds several minutes or more to our response time as personnel must respond to the station, pick up the appropriate
  36|                equipment and then respond the call. In addition, our volume of calls for assistance shows no sign of abating. These factors lead me to
  37|                continue to call for 24 hour staffing in the Station. Providing funding to staff two Firefighter/EMT's overnight and on weekends would
  38|                allow either the ambulance or first piece of fire apparatus to respond immediately. Reducing the response time by four or more minutes is
  39|                critical in cases of serious medical emergencies or structure fires. This staffing would in no way reduce the need for our staff of on call
  40|                personnel; it would only supplement their response and reduce response times. In fact, the current recommendation is to use our
  41|                existing personnel on a per diem basis to provide that staffing.
  42|                           Our firefighters continue to train for the myriad of situations which can be encountered even in a town such as ours. Today's
  43|                firefighter must be skilled in not only firefighting, but medical care, hazardous materials mitigation and technical rescue. Our EMT's must
  44|                continue to stay abreast of the latest treatment protocols and advances in lifesaving skills. Personnel train regularly both here at the
  45|                Station and at classes sponsored by other agencies. While they are compensated for some of the training, the vast majority is done on
  46|                their own time. To give an example, a new Firefighter/EMT must attend over 340 hours of basic firefighting and Emergency Medical
  47|               Technician training. Once they are certified, they must complete over 50 hours of continuing education and refresher training every two
  48|                                                                                               21

===PAGE 25=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|               years, just for their EMT. Operators of Fire Apparatus must complete an additional 65 hours of training in pump and aerial operations,
   2|               hydraulics calculations and vehicle operation.
   3|                          Several of our personnel are members of Regional Technical Rescue units, providing services for Confined Space and Cave In
   4|               Rescue, as well as Underwater Search and Recovery. They stand ready to respond as needed throughout Northern Worcester County.
   5|                          2013 continued to be an active year for our Fire Prevention efforts.              By law we are required to inspect Smoke and Carbon
   6|               Monoxide Detector installations when a home is sold, as well as all new oil burner and propane gas installations. Any business holding a
   7|               liquor license is also required to be inspected for fire safety issues as well. In addition, all new construction projects must have their
   8|               plans reviewed for proper fire protection, as well as ongoing inspections during the construction process. Personnel also continue to
   9|               provide fire safety education to not only children in our schools but adults as well. In late 2013, a $4900 Grant was received through the
  10|               Department of Fire Services to assist with these fire safety initiatives. Several members are also Certified First Aid and CPR Instructors and provide training to both citizens and town employees.
  11|                          The transition of our Communications Center to the Nashoba Valley Dispatch Center was completed on June 18, 2013. This
  12|               center now handles all of the 911 calls and public safety communications for our town, as well as the communities of Devens, Harvard
  13|               and Lancaster. The Center is staffed with 2-3 Dispatchers 24 hours day. This will allow for better provision of medical pre-arrival
  14|               assistance, as well as decreased call processing time and more efficient emergency communications.
  15|                          Two Federal Grants were received totalling nearly $54,000. These Grants provided the following to the Fire Department. Two 700lb. rated power lift stretchers for the Ambulances, along with related equipment. These stretchers will
  16|                                          make it easier and safer for our EMT's to move patients, and drastically reduce the chance of back injuries.
  17|                                         "Bailout Escape Kits" for all Firefighting personnel- These kits consist of a rapelling type harness integrated into a
  18|                                          Firefighter's Turnout Gear, along with 50' of rope and a hook. It allows a firefighter to perform an emergency
  19|                                          rappel up to 4-5 stories in height in the event they become trapped or cut off by fire. This equipment is actually
  20|                                          required by law in some states. Training to use the equipment was completed in December of 2013 and it was
  21|                                          placed in service.
  22|                                          Trauma Equipment and kits- An Emergency Preparedness Grant allowed the purchase of 4 specialized medical
  23|                                          kits. These kits are specifically configured to deal with injuries resulting from explosions and penetrating injuries
  24|                                          such as gunshot wounds. As the Marathon Bombing Incident taught us, this kind of equipment can save countless
  25|                                          lives if the unthinkable does occur.
  26|                          Our Department continues to actively pursue Grant Funding opportunities as they occur.
  27|               repairs will hopefully extend this truck's lifespan another 5-6 years.
  28|                          The replacement for Rescue 2 has been ordered and we expect delivery in mid-2014.                          This new unit will replace a 2004
  29|               ambulance and will become the primary ambulance. Rescue 1 will then be rotated to backup status. We strive to maintain a replacement
  30|                cycle of 5-6 years on our primary ambulance and 10-12 years on the backup unit.
  31|                          Funding from the 2013 Town Meeting also allowed us to purchase harnesses to complement the "bailout kits" purchased
  32|               through the grant. It also allowed us to replace 12 sets of turnout gear.
  33|                           Firefighters Eric Bursey, Paul A. Duquette, Carl Gamberdella, James Reynolds and lan Reynolds graduated from the
  34|                Massachusetts Firefighting Academy's Call/Volunteer Training Program. This intensive 6 month program provides the basic skills
  35|                firefighters need to operate in today's challenging environments. All newly hired Firefighters are required to complete this program within
  36|                one year of hire. In addition, Firefighter Carl Gamberdella was the first Firefighter from Lunenburg to receive the Martin H. McNamara
  37|                Award. This award recognizes the top overall student in each Training Class, and is named for a Call Firefighter from Lancaster who was
  38|               killed in the Line of Duty in 2003.
  39|                          In the fall of 2013, Fire Lieutenant Scott Dillon and Firefighter Matthew Glenny, along with Police Officers Charles Deming, Peter
  40|                Lekaditis and Devon O'Brien were recognized for their sucessful rescue of a person trapped in a pickup truck submerged in over 10 feet
  41|                of water. The rescue was performed without benefit of protective equipment or SCUBA equipment and was truly a heroic act worthy of
  42|               recognition.
  43|                          In conclusion, I would like to thank the members of the Lunenburg Fire Department for their outstanding commitment to the
  44|                Town. Their enthusiasm, professionalism and dedication continue to impress me every day. I would also like to thank the other
  45|                department heads, boards and commissions for their continuing help. Most people do not realize the level of interdepartmental
  46|                                                                                              22

  17|                                                                         Department Mission Statement
  18|               The mission of the Lunenburg Police department is defined by a more advanced style of policing that emphasizes
  19|               personal service and community problem resolution, and accentuates the uncompromising integrity, trust, respect, and
  20|               accountability, of the Police Officers who are committed to providing a safe and secure environment for every citizen.
  21|               The men and women of the Lunenburg Police Department are required to accept, support, and carry out this mission in a
  22|               manner consistent with our core values and with distinction given all practical resources in the interest of preventing
  23|              crime, preserving peace, and the protection of life and property.

   1|                                                                           SUPERINTENDENT'S MESSAGE
   2|                                                                         Loxi Jo Calmes, Superintendent of Schools
   3|                          This has been another engaging and productive year for the Lunenburg Public Schools.                            We were elated with the
   4|                          overwhelming support of the community for the new middle/high school project. On behalf of our students and school !
   5|                          want to again thank the community for their support of the project. In April the project's consultant team submitted design
   6|                          development documents for Massachusetts School Building Authority (MSBA) review. Site enabling is scheduled to begin
   7|                          summer 2014 with building construction to take place fall 2014 to summer 2016. The anticipated opening of the new school
   8|                         is September 2016. Follow the building project at our website (www.lunenburgschools.net).
   9|                          Again this year I want to congratulate the students, their parents, the faculty and staff for the impressive state assessment
  10|                          results again this year.    The overall performance on the state assessment (MCAS) is quite positive. We are very proud of
  11|                          all our Lunenburg students and most appreciative of the adults -teachers, parents/guardians, support personnel and
  12|                                               lo engage, supperost appreciative of the adults -teachers, at the tenth grade leve
  13|                          posted strong performance at the 8th grade in English language arts with 83% of students advanced or proficient. In
  14|                          mathematics 80% of students at grades 3 and 6 scored advanced or proficient. The faculties in every school will continue to
  15|                          analyze results in order to adjust curriculum and instruction to align with the common core of learning outcomes measured
  16|                          by the state assessment.
  17|                          Lunenburg students continue to excel both in and outside the classroom. I am impressed by the passion, talents, tenacity
  18|                          and discipline of our students, musicians, and athletes. Various individuals and academic teams scored high marks in a
  19|                          variety of state and national contests. School thespians entertained us with a production of "It's A Wonderful Life: A Live
  20|                          Radio Play" and the forty-eight annual competitive class plays were outstanding. The marching band performed amazing
  21|                          half-time shows, and the middle and high school music concerts were equally impressive and uplifting. Chorus members
  22|                          were invited to perform at Symphony Hall with the Handel and Haydn Society. Lunenburg students' work in the visual arts
  23|                         was exhibited locally and at state competitions. Our athletic teams competed successfully at the district and state levels.
  24|                          The Lunenburg Public Schools, like all schools across the Commonwealth, are focused on implementation of the new
  25|                          statewide educator evaluation model. The District receives funds under the Race to the Top Grant, implementation of the
  26|                          model began last year with about 35% of educators and 100% of the administrators being evaluated under the new model.
  27|                          This year 100% of educators are being evaluated under the new model. The new educator evaluation model was designed
  28|                          to promote educator growth and development and to ensure that every student has a highly accomplished teacher, and
  29|                          every school an effective leader. This new educator evaluation system is based upon a continuous improvement model and
  30|                          recognizes excellence in teaching and leading.
  31|                          I was pleased to recognize Alaina Johansson as this year's Superintendent's Award recipient. In addition to having
  32|                          distinguished herself as a scholar, Alaina has also been recognized for leadership and service. Throughout her high school
  33|                          career she managed a rigorous class schedule while participating in athletics and several other extracurricular activities. I
  34|                          also want to acknowledge Eighth grader Jack Meilleur who represented our community at Governor Patrick's Project 351 -
  35|                         2014 day of service. These students, along with many others across the district, continue to serve as models of responsible
  36|                         community membership. This fall Alaina led an impressive effort to collect school supplies for Lunenburg students. Across
  37|                          the district our students continue to collect for the town food bank and raise awareness and funds for many other worthy
  38|                          This past year our schools bid farewell to four retirees who devoted many years to public service. Retiring this past year
  39|                          were Arline Rogers, instructional aide, with 11 years of service; DonnaLee Duquette, instructional tutor, with 19 years of
  40|                          service; Nancy Strom, Technology Director with 3 years of service; and Helena Louzonis, high school science teacher with 7
  41|                          years of service. We appreciate the many contributions of these dedicated and committed professionals and extend our
  42|                          best wishes to them in their retirement.

  33|                                                                                       SCHOOL FACILITIES
  34|                                                                               John Londa, Director of Facilities
  35|                             Planning for replacement of Lunenburg High School and Thomas C. Passios Elementary School continued following the
  36|                             passage of the proposal to construct a 169,000 square foot middle school / high school building in January 2014. The
  37|                             project budget was set at $72, 975,321 for design and construction plus contingencies. Also in January, the School Building
  38|                             Committee approved an amendment to continue with Joslin, Lesser's contract as Owner's Project Manager (OPM) for
  39|                             detailed Design and Construction of the school. Tappe Associates continues on in the design process with an anticipated
  40|                             ground breaking for fall 2014. In July 2013, the School Building Committee, in conjunction with the OPM and architects,
  41|                             selected Shawmut Design and Construction Inc. to serve as the Construction Manager at Risk for the project.
  42|                             The FY14 capital plan approved upgrades to the front end controllers of the Building Management Systems at Lunenburg
  43|                             High School, Turkey Hill Middle School and T.C.               Passios Elementary School at a total cost of $40,378. An asbestos
  44|                             abatement project for repairs at the high school and the Passios Elementary school was also funded at $15,000.
  45|                             Remediation and air testing were completed in December 2013 by Abide Inc and Cardno ATC respectively. Also funded in
  46|                             the FY14 Capital Plan was $16,000 to replace the linoleum flooring of the cafeteria and teacher lunch room at Lunenburg
  47|                             Primary School. The original linoleum floor failed prematurely due to high moisture and poor adhesion of the floor to the
  48|                             cement slab. In February 2014, Monument Flooring Inc. replaced the floor at a cost of $14,950. Armstrong Flooring Inc.
  49|                                                                                                 30

===PAGE 34=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                        The focus of the school maintenance effort for FY14 concentrated on preventative maintenance of heat and ventilation
   2|                        systems and energy conservation. The Town of Lunenburg successfully applied as a Green Community in 2013 which will
   3|                        allow funding for energy conservation measures such as lighting improvements, tightening of building envelopes, and
   4|                        improvements to building operations. Projects that were identified for Turkey Hill Middle School and Lunenburg Primary
   5|                        School as part of the energy reduction plan are expected to be completed in 2015. Conversion to LED technology for
   6|                        lighting was a major component of the energy conservation measures identified.
   7|                        In September 2013, the School Department adopted an online facilities scheduling system in order to streamline procedures,
   8|                        allow system wide availability of facility scheduling, and provide feedback to requesters about their requests.
   9|                        For the FY15 Capital Program, the School Department requested $14,000 to upgrade the front end controller of the HVAC
  10|                        control system at Lunenburg Primary School as its highest priority. A request for $138,000 was submitted for the first phase
  11|                        of a two phase abatement of spray on asbestos fireproofing above the ceilings at Turkey Hill Middle School. The asbestos
  12|                        remediation will require removal and replacement of ceilings at the Middle School as they will be considered contaminated
  13|                        with asbestos. Haynes Lieneck and Smith Inc. will prepare drawings for the work and Cardno ATC will be the Asbestos
  14|                        Planner for this project. A request was also submitted for a shade structure and outdoor classroom for Turkey Hill Middle
  15|                        School at a projected cost of $41,766 and $65,000 for system wide technology upgrades.

  19|                                   There are two hundred and ninety-two students with disabilities receiving special education or related services in
  20|                        Lunenburg. This represents 18.4% of our total student population of 1, 587 students. This percentage is slightly more than
  21|                        the state average of 17%. Of the total number of students receiving services, twenty-two students received services outside
  22|                        the district during the 2012/2013 school year. Of the twenty-two students, seventeen are in day placements and five are in
  23|                        residential placements.         Of the day placements, five are serviced in Collaboratives and the rest are in private day
  24|                       placements. Ninety-three students identified with a disability receive accommodations via 504 plans in Lunenburg.
  25|                                   This year as a district, our Accountability and Assistance Level is Level 2 with both Turkey Hill Middle and
  26|                        Lunenburg High Schools listed as Level 2. The state report card lists Students with Disabilities in Lunenburg as "Meets
  27|                       Requirements-At Risk (MRAR)". The district's Students with Disabilities progress towards narrowing proficiency gaps
  28|                       [Cumulative Progress and Performance Index (CPI): 1-100)] is listed at 50 with the target set for 75. One hundred percent of
  29|                      Students with Disabilities participated in MCAS this year and the target was met. See school/district profiles at:
