===PAGE 36=== (ocr, 53 lines)
   1|                                                                                      PRIMARY SCHOOL
   2|                                                                                     Elaine Blaisdell, Principal
   3|                                      The Lunenburg Primary School is a child-centered school devoted to providing quality early childhood education
   4|                           for all students. We are a community of teachers, students, and parents working together to create a safe and respectful
   5|                           environment for children. Current knowledge and research of child development guides the school's beliefs and practices
   6|                           and provides the foundation for learning for all students.
   7|                                      For the last two years the school serviced Preschool to Third grade students. The February 20, 2014 student count
   8|                           reported to the state was 517 students. Our Preschool program services 19 three year old students and thirty-three four
   9|                           year old students. We have five Kindergarten classes servicing one hundred and twenty-three students. The class sizes in
  10|                           our Kindergarten programs range from 24-25 students. First grade has one hundred and thirteen students with class sizes
  11|                           ranging from 21-22. Second grade has one hundred and twenty-five students and the third grade has one hundred and four
  12|                           students. We are utilizing all available space in the building to provide a child centered enriching educational program for
  13|                           all our students.
  14|                                      We constantly strive to find better ways to meet the collective needs as well as the individual needs of our
  15|                           students. Our Professional Development focused on Race to the Top requirements including the new Teacher Evaluation,
  16|                           and the Common Core curriculum. All of our classroom teachers continued to use Responsive Classroom strategies as part
  17|                           of our Academic/Social/Emotional Learning Curriculum which includes morning meetings, guided discovery, rules and
  18|                        / logical consequences, and academic choice.
  19|                                      The Primary School community continues to strive toward academic excellence. Common assessments are in
  20|                           place in the fall, winter, and spring for all children in grades K-3 in the areas of reading, writing, and math. These common
  21|                           assessments serve to monitor the effectiveness of teacher instruction and drive instruction. We are now able to track
  22|                           progress throughout the year in a faster, more efficient manner in order to improve instruction. Additional resources and
  23|                           direct instruction are provided to address the areas of concern identified through these common assessments. The Primary
  24|                           School is a Title One school and we have continued our tiered instructional system to insure that all students have the
  25|                           services they need to be successful. Tiered instructional services are provided in the area of Language Arts and Math. The
  26|                           first tier is the largest, and is fitted for universal instruction in the classroom. Teachers are skilled in differentiating
  27|                           instruction to meet a large number of student needs in the classroom. The second tier is based on student needs that are
  28|                           targeted, individual, and involve more intensive interventions. The third tier is the smallest and involves special services
  29|                           with very intense interventions. The tiered instructional model requires that the teachers not only meet the needs of the
  30|                           students who may be struggling with learning but those students who are doing well and need to strive to achieve higher
  31|                           levels of learning. Learning is becoming more personalized and students are being challenged to be independent learners
  32|                           and problem solvers.
  33|                                      Our third graders participated in the Spring 2013 MCAS Assessment. For our 3rd graders this was their first
  34|                           experience taking the high stakes state testing. We work to ensure that students understand that they are capable and
  35|                           prepared to take this assessment. We talk a lot about the learning they have experienced over the last several years and
  36|                           how this has prepared them to perform well on this assessment.
  37|                                  Grade 3 ELA                 Advanced               Proficient           Needs Improvement                Warning
  38|                              Number of Students                  20                      60                        38                         2
  39|                             Percent of Students                              65%                                    31%                      2%
  40|                                 Grade 3 Math                Advanced                Proficient           Needs Improvement               Warning
  41|                             Number of Students                   45                      52                          5                        6
  42|                              Percent of Students                37%                     43%                       13%                       5%
  43|                           Compared to last year, fewer students in this cohort of students scored in the proficient and advanced ranges of functioning
  44|                           in the area of ELA. There was an 11% drop compared to last year, resulting in a higher number of students scoring in the
  45|                           needs improvement range.
  46|                           Last year 85% of the students who took the Math MCAS scored in the Advanced/Proficient range of functioning. Although
  47|                        there was a slight decrease overall in the Advanced/Proficient range this year compared to last year, 10% more students
  48|                           scored in the advanced range of functioning in the area of Math (last year 27% Advanced, 47% proficient).
  49|                           Our staff works hard to maintain a school culture which is positive, inclusive, and supportive of each of our members. We
  50|                           have high expectations, insist on respectful behavior, and recognize the importance of building a community where
  51|                           members feel a sense of belonging and responsibility to contribute. Our students have a right to be in an environment
  52|                           which is safe, promotes learning, and enables them to achieve success.

   4|                             Principal:                                Elaine E. Blaisdell

   1|                         Students also receive instruction in physical education, art, library media, and music classes that allows them to explore their
   2|                         strengths and talents. Students also have the opportunity to sign up for and participate in band and chorus during this block.
   3|                         This enrichment block is in addition to the traditional special area classes students receive, and this block offers students
   4|                        additional physical movement throughout the day, additional opportunities for hands on activities and more exposure to the
   5|                        arts, theatre and technology throughout the school day.
   6|                         We make a positive impact on the students we serve by offering a variety of different programs developed to promote social
   7|                         and emotional growth, self-awareness, and community service. We continue to utilize the Developmental Design program to
   8|                         grades six and seven to deliver our social emotional curriculum. This program works by students meeting with teachers in
   9|                        small groups to teach social responsibility and problem solving, as well as developing empathy and promoting positive self-
  10|                        esteem. Grades four and five continue to utilize Responsive Classroom strategies, which deliver a similar social emotional
  11|                        curriculum with similar positive results. We continue to refine and enhance our programs to incorporate anti-bullying lessons.
  12|                        We utilize best practices such as peer mediation and the Big Sibs program to foster and develop positive peer leadership
  13|                        skills and effective peer mentoring. All of the social emotional programs have been developed to allow students to showcase
  14|                        their talents and abilities, grow and improve as individuals, and promote community outreach for the younger generation.
  15|                        Turkey Hill Middle school continues to provide educational services and support that ensures student success and promotes
  16|                        lifelong learning and responsible community membership. The key components to our success is our talented faculty
  17|                        committed to making a positive impact on students, our parents and community who support our teaching and learning, and
  18|                        most of all, our students who inspire us through their commitment to learning. Together we continue to make the difference in
  19|                        the lives of students.

  12|                                                                                     HIGH SCHOOL
  13|                                                                                 Brian Spadafino, Principal
  14|                       Lunenburg High School was a hub for academic and social activity in 2013-2014. There was more stability within the faculty
  15|                       and student body, at the beginning of the school year. In the 2012-2013 school year, we welcomed almost 250 eighth and
  16|                       ninth grade students and eight faculty members to Lunenburg High School. This year, we only had to orient 145 eighth grade
  17|                       students, one teacher, and four staff members to our building and school culture. With the assistance of 50 student leaders,
  18|                       we successfully transitioned the eighth grade students into the LHS culture by having an eighth grade orientation on the first
  19|                       day of school. We opened the year, as we do every year, with a guest speaker from the Worcester County District Attorney's
  20|                       Office to discuss responsible Internet use, Facebook, cyber-bullying, and Internet safety with our incoming students and
  21|                       parents.
  22|                       NEASC ACCREDITATION
  23|                       Lunenburg High School recently completed a Special Progress Report for the Commission on Public Secondary Schools of
  24|                       the New England Association of Schools and Colleges. In 2010, the Commission recommended that the school receive
  25|                       continued accreditation in the Association. The Commission's decision was based upon review of an evaluation report
  26|                       prepared by a visiting committee in April 2010. The Two Year Progress Report, submitted in January, 2013 received high
  27|                       marks from the Commission reviewing our submission.
  28|                       While the Commission lauded LHS in many areas, the Commission warned LHS in three areas and asked for a Special
  29|                       Progress Report to be completed. Of major concern were issues with the facility and meeting 21st century learning
  30|                       expectations. The Commission stated that the physical plant significantly limits the school's ability to meet the educational
  31|                       needs of students, and that there is an expectation to develop both a short- and long-term plan to fund and address the roof and window issues, limited handicap
  32|                        significant facilities concerns including infrastructure and wiring issues,
  33|                        accessibility and inadequate space. In curriculum, the commission recommended the further development of a standard
  34|                       template to clearly articulate school-wide expectations for student learning.
  35|                        As part of the Special Progress Report, the leadership team was charged with addressing the recommendations that were
  36|                        made by the Commission. Of the three Highlighted recommendations, one was deemed "complete" and two were deemed "in
  37|                        progress." The "completed" recommendation was due to the fact that On January 7, 2014, Lunenburg voters supported the
  38|                       design of a grade 6-12, middle/high school building project, at a Special Town Meeting. On January 11, 2014, the town of
  39|                        Lunenburg voted to approve a ballot proposal to build a new middle/high school. Voters cast 1,414 votes in favor of the
  40|                        project and only 543 votes were in opposition to the project, in the special election. The total cost of the project is
  41|                        $72,975,321. The leadership team reworked the school-wide learning expectations so that they are aligned with the Common
  42|                        Core State Standards. The report was submitted to the Commission on February 4, 2014.
  43|                        SCHOOL/COMMUNITY PARTNERSHIPS
  44|                        In January, a group of students came together to look at how school culture at LHS could be improved. The group met three
  45|                        days after school. They decided that the best way to positively impact school culture is to: put together a group of students
  46|                        from each grade level to help acclimate new students to LHS; start a student-led newspaper to expose students to what is
  47|                        going on in school and after-school; and look at offering more extra-curricular activities that focus on student interests. The
  48|                                                                                                37

===PAGE 41=== (ocr, 46 lines)
   1|                         culture committee believes by exposing students to things they may be interested in and other students who share similar
   2|                         interests, students will feel a stronger connection to school.
   3|                         In March, the National Honor Society will be having its annual Blood Drive. Students sixteen and over will be able to
   4|                         participate in the Blood Drive with parent permission.

  27|                        We were also very pleased to learn that 31 seniors at Lunenburg High School received the state's John and Abigail Adams
  28|                        Scholarship this year. The scholarship is available to students whose MCAS performance puts them at the top 25 percent of
  29|                       their district. To be eligible, students must either score Advanced on one exam and Proficient on the other, or Advanced on
  30|                        both the English and Math exams.
  31|                       HOMECOMING, ATHLETICS AND EXTRACURRICULAR ACTIVITIES
  32|                        Homecoming was once again a great town-wide celebration, culminating with a beautiful parade complete with floats
  33|                       representing all four high school classes. Many of our sports teams qualified for tournament and district play, and several
  34|                        school records in track were shattered by our talented student-athletes. LHS received a Sportsmanship Award from the MIAA
  35|                        because we did not have any of our players or coaches ejected from an athletic event. Mock trial, Latin club, marching band,
  36|                       jazz band and the math team represented HS in numerous competitions; the 48th annual Competitive Class Plays delighted
  37|                       the audience, and the school-wide play, It's a Wonderful Life: The Live Radio Version, was well received. The Pops and Senior
  38|                       Showcase are scheduled for the spring. Members of the LHS chorus had the opportunity to sing at Symphony Hall with the
  39|                       Handel and Hayden Society and seven other Massachusetts high schools.
  40|                       COMMUNITY BUILDING
  41|                       National Honor Society successfully supported the "Giftmart" drive during the holidays, helping to provide joy to needy
  42|                       children and families in conjunction with local agencies. The World Hunger Task Force will be raising money through Penny
  43|                       Wars this spring. The money will be donated toward the fight against hunger. Also, this winter, one of our students
  44|                       participated in Project 351. He spent the day in Boston with hundreds of eighth grade students, giving back to the community.
  45|                       Last summer, another student and a maintenance worker painted the LHS gymnasium.

   1|                                       MONTACHUSETT REGIONAL VOCATIONAL TECHNICAL SCHOOL
   2|                     As I enter into my third year as Superintendent-Director of Monty Tech, I remain humbled and honored to represent a
   3|                     school that continues to transform secondary education in North Central Massachusetts. Compiling information
   4|                     for the 2013 Annual Report has provided me with an opportunity to reflect on the achievements and activities of our
   5|                     students, faculty and staff. The 2012-2013 school year brought many exciting opportunities for the Monty Tech
   6|                     educational community. It was a banner year, marked by student achievement, faculty and staff distinctions, and
   7|                    leadership development, including:
   8|                     • Competing in three categories: network security, digital forensics, and Cisco networking, a team of six talented
   9|                           students captured second place at the CyberPatriot event, a national high school cyber defense competition,
  10|                          founded by the Air Force Association.
  11|                       Students at Montachusett Regional Vocational                          Technical School continued to demonstrate high
  12|                           academic achievement. In the spring of 2012, Monty Tech's passing rate on the MCAS English Language
  13|                           Arts exam was 99%, Mathematics 98%, and Biology 97%.
  14|                           experiment launch into space, providing students with an opportunity to study the effects of gravity on their
  15|                          innovative science projects.
  16|                        Mass Audubon and the New England Farm and Garden Associations recognized our talented science
  17|                          instructor, Mr. Ken Pearson, with the well-deserved the 2013 Conservation Teacher of the Year Award.
  18|                     • Monty Tech administrators made the new educator evaluation system a priority, attended a wide array of
  19|                           professional development programs, and continue to lead efforts to bring innovative and meaningful change
  20|                          to the complex system.
  21|                     Today, the Monty Tech model of integrated academic and career-focused learning continues to provide high quality
  22|                     education to area youth. A Monty Tech education not only prepares students for employment, but also for post-
  23|                     secondary education.          In fact, more than 60% of the Class of 2013 entered college or an advanced training
  24|                     program upon graduation. But what about success and retention in these post-secondary programs?
  25|                     Because Monty Tech administrators, faculty and staff have a vested interest in the success of our students, the
  26|                     District has taken measures to keep in touch with our program graduates. One year after leaving Monty Tech, the
  27|                     graduates of 2012 reported the following:
  28|                               35% are currently employed in a field related to their Monty Tech trade
  29|                               10% are employed in a field unrelated to their vocational education
  30|                               49% are currently pursuing a higher education
  31|                          :    4% have elected to serve in the United States military
  32|                   From a fiscal perspective, Monty Tech, like other schools in your community, is dependent on public support
  33|                   Though the District has been impacted by the nationwide economic recession, we have made every effort to retain a
  34|                   technology and 215t Century thinking.
  35|                    The District's FY13 Annual Budget was accepted by each of the eighteen member communities, and reflected a
  36|                     7% increase over the 2011-2012 Educational Plan. We recognize that we are in the midst of some of the most difficult
  37|                     economic times faced by public education and, as a result, we will continue to strive to seek creative ways to
  38|                     minimize member-town assessments.
  39|                                                                                                          Respectfully submitted,
  40|                                                                                                                            C. Saut

  22|                                                                                            Enrollment
  23|                         On October 1, 2012, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,432 students in grades nine through twelve. Students
  24|                         are represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (58), Ashby (37), Athol (110), Barre (39),
  25|                         Fitchburg (376), Gardner (173), Harvard (4), Holden (60), Hubbardston (56), Lunenburg (72), Petersham (8),
  26|                         Phillipston (17), Princeton (14), Royalston (24), Sterling (58), Templeton (110), Westminster (70), and Winchendon
  27|                         (127). The remaining nineteen students were from out-of-district towns, including Clinton, Hardwick, Leominster,
  28|                         Orange, Townsend and Worcester.
  29|                         Throughout 2012-2013, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community members to
  30|                         learn about and visit the school. In October 2012, approximately 1,000 district eighth graders participated in the
  31|                         annual "Tour Day" event. Students toured              our twenty-vocational/technical areas and learned about the school's
  32|                         challenging academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered
  33|                        interested   students the opportunity to return in the evening with their family members, to further explore the
  34|                        facilities and talk with staff members.
  35|                            Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for
  36|                         The balance of the applications came from students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because there are only a limited number of students the school can accept each year, 379 freshmen and 22 upperclassmen
  37|                         were admitted.

   3|                                                                                   Class of 2012 Awards
   4|                       Members of the Class of 2013 were awarded                       approximately $50,000 in scholarships.                The Monty Tech
   5|                       Foundation generously provided $27,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $250 to
   6|                                   The Foundation also awarded $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state
   7|                       organizations, as well as generous individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech
   8|                       graduates in the form of financial donations. The School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates

  10|                       Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher education.
  11|                       Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits                             through a number of articulation
  12|                       agreements with public and private colleges across the country. Well over 60% of the graduating class of 2013 reported
  13|                       plans to enroll at either a 2-year college, 4-year college/university, or a technical/trade school upon graduation. By
  14|                       earning college credits while still in high school, these students will benefit by saving both time and money as they
  15|                       pursue advanced educational programs.
  16|                                                                                     Financial Report
  17|                      In an effort to develop a cost-effective budget for the fiscal year 2012-2013, a great deal of effort was put forth by
  18|                      the School Committee, administration and staff. The final fiscal year 2012-2013 Educational Plan totaled $24,333,209,
  19|                       which represents a 7.0% increase over the 2011-2012 Educational Plan. The District held spending in fiscal year
  20|                       2012-2013 to the minimum required by Massachusetts General Law Chapter 70.
  21|                       The District was audited in August 2013 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of
  22|                       Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and a very good report is anticipated.
  23|                                                                                 Grants and Contracts
  24|                       Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds assist in providing many
  25|                      educational and social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2013, state and federal grant sources
  26|                      provided the school with $922,132. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services, Social
  27|                      Intervention and Mediation, Improving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title
  28|                      I Support, Perkins Occupational Education, and Summer Academic Support.
  29|                      In addition to state and federal grant sources, Monty Tech was also the recipient of private and competitive grant
  30|                      awards totaling $358,245. Included in that total are a number of highly competitive awards, including a $248,275
  31|                      award from the Massachusetts Life Sciences Center, used to renovate two science laboratories, and a $100,000
  32|                      award from the Executive Office for Administration and Finance, used to purchase vocational-technical
  33|                      training equipment, specifically a Sinker Electrical Discharge Machine (EDM), which will be used to train our Machine
  34|                      Technology students, creating a more skilled and better prepared regional workforce. Grants totaled $1,280,377 for
  35|                      fiscal year 2013.

  22|                                                              Vocational Projects in the District Communities
  23|                         Unlike students in traditional, comprehensive high schools,                     students at Monty Tech are asked to put their
  24|                         education into practice on a daily basis. Students across the twenty different vocational technical education programs
  25|                         are building homes, reconstructing damaged properties, repairing service vehicles, making walkways more
  26|                         accessible, and performing countless community services.
  27|                         The 2012-2013 school year was a busy one for our vocational educators, as each trade aimed to provide
  28|                        practical, hands-on learning experiences for our students, while helping to improve each of the eighteen member
  29|                         communities.
  30|                         Auto Body: Collision Repair Technology: During 2012-2013, the program was fortunate to receive equipment
  31|                         upgrades including a new aluminum pulse welder, and an assortment of hand and air tools. The program introduced
  32|                         students to a virtual 3-D spray system, designed to provide students with the opportunity to practice the technique
  33|                         painting without using any materials,              and the program's waterborne paint spray booth continues to provide
  34|                         students with training with more efficient waterborne paint systems. Throughout the year, the program received
  35|                         over eighty requests for service and repair work, including a number of requests from local school, police and
  36|                         fire departments.      Students and instructors refinished a Westminster Police Department Speed Trailer, completed
  37|                         numerous projects in support of the NEADS Canines for Combat Veterans program, as well as repaired a Gardner
  38|                         Fire Department vehicle. (Total enrollment: 63; 42 males, 21 females)
  39|                         Automotive Technology: The Automotive Technology program experienced instructional transition, as long-time
  40|                                      Ken Baer accepted            another position within the District.                   George Russell, veteran automotive
  41|                        technician, filled the vacant position and has proven to be a valuable contributor in the department. Two of our
  42|                        top students, Seniors Hunter Manley and Mike Pauplis placed fourth at the state Ford AAA Automotive
  43|                                            Several students performed extremely well at the district level SkillsUSA competition. Hunter
  44|                         Manley of Phillipston, advanced to the state competition, where he was awarded a gold medal for his

   1|                         performance and qualified for the national competition in Kansas City, MO. The program also supported eight
   2|                         students in the Co-Operative Education program. These students gained valuable workplace experience, applying
   3|                         the skills they learned in the rigorous automotive technology program, and received high praise from their
   4|                         employers. (Total enrollment: 53; 44 males, 9 females)
   5|                         Business Technology: Students and instructors in the Business Technology continue to enjoy an increased presence
   6|                        in the school community, providing assistance to shops and offices throughout the school by coordinating bulk
   7|                         mailing, labeling, organizing, and collating jobs. Offices that receive direct support from the Business Technology
   8|                         program include:        Monty Tech Nurses' Office, front office, and Student Support Services. Students also assist
   9|                         wherever there is a customer service need, as in the annual greenhouse project, directed by Mr. Dylan Hager.
  10|                         Students serve as customer service representatives and cashiers for this very busy seasonal operation.                               Students

  12|                         marketing. (Total enrollment: 76; 15 males, 61 females)

  22|                        45 males, 27 females)
  23|                        Cosmetology: Providing students with educational space that closely reflects industry trends and a typical workplace
  24|                        environment are paramount in the cosmetology program. In November 2012, the renovation of rooms #202 and
  25|                        #210 was completed, offering students and instructors additional opportunities to train in a setting that closely
  26|                        resembles a traditional salon. The new space includes: twenty-four dry stations, twelve manicure tables and stools,
  27|                        new student lockers, shelving, wig warmers, new electrical outlets and fresh paint.                                Each of the programs
  28|                        twenty-two seniors successfully obtained their cosmetology license before graduation- bringing the programs total
  29|                        number of licensed operators to 225 (and three manicurists) to date. Monty Tech cosmetology instructors continued to
  30|                        promote community service, encouraging all students to participate in meaningful activities at the Gardner High Rise.
  31|                        Students provide free hair and nail services to the elderly once a month, a program that began in 1998. (Total
  32|                        enrollment: 89; 1 male, 88 females)

  36|                                                                                       Special Services
  37|                          During the 2012-2013 school year, Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District provided special
  38|                           services to approximately three hundred students - measuring progress of approximately two hundred students on
  39|                           Individual Education Plans (EPs) and just under one hundred students adhering to individualized Section 504 plans.
  40|                           While the Student Support Services (SSS) Department encompasses special education, the department provides
  41|                           support and is available to all Monty Tech students.
  42|                           The department includes a full-time nursing staff that administers medications, performs state-mandated health
  43|                           screening exams, and provides, when necessary, health information to the special education team for a student's lEP
  44|                          meeting.      The department is fortunate to be able to support a full-time school social worker who participates in
  45|                          departmental meetings and assists students who have needs concerning finances, family issues, homelessness,
  46|                                                                                  and clothing. The school is fortunate to have on staff a full-time
  47|                          psychologist, whose role it is to evaluate all students referred for an initial evaluation or who require a three-year
  48|                          re-evaluation. In addition, we have a full-time speech pathologist, who is available to assist students with
  49|                          disabilities, assess these students and consult with teachers. Our students also have access to the services of a
  50|                          full-time adjustment counselor and part-time school psychologist. All of these individuals are available for
  51|                          scheduled counseling sessions and mental health emergency treatment, as well as crisis intervention.
  52|                                                                                                48

===PAGE 52=== (ocr, 50 lines)
   1|                     The school's Director of Student Support Services oversees the district's Special Education Program, which is
   2|                    reviewed annually in May, in accordance with regulatory requirements. The comprehensive review and evaluation
   3|                     are done in collaboration with the Parent Advisory Council, and the results of the evaluation are used to improve
   4|                     the special education procedures and programs in place at Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical
   5|                     School.
   6|                                                                                      Technology
   7|                     In 2012-2013 Monty Tech began the process of improving our network infrastructure to meet the needs of the
   8|                     future. The Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education's initiative to replace the current
   9|                     MCAS student assessment testing system with PARCC, an online student assessment testing system, is the driving
  10|                     force for such improvements in school districts across the state.
  11|                     The network core switch has been upgraded along with other network switches throughout the building. Internet
  12|                     bandwidth was increased considerably, and the school selected a wireless network vendor to install building-wide
  13|                     wireless access during the summer of 2013. We believe these improvements to the school's infrastructure have
  14|                     prepared the school for the technology-driven PARCC assessment.
  15|                     Monty Tech continues its participation in the Massachusetts Office of Health and Human Services Virtual Gateway
  16|                    initiative, which provides direct certification of free and reduced lunch students. This process has improved the
  17|                     process of qualifying students for subsidized meals, directly impacting technology costs at the school. As the
  18|                     number of students who qualify for subsidized meals increases, so too, does our e-rate discount, substantially
  19|                     reducing costs associated with the District's internet, firewall, email systems.
  20|                     Monty Tech continues to replace and add technology equipment and software so that students gain experience
  21|                     with newer technology. Our Information Technology program added a virtual server, and several of our other shops
  22|                     replaced equipment that is now based on computer technology.
  23|                                                                                      Skills USA

  25|                     workforce.    Through our association with SkillsUSA, our students develop job skills, leadership and professional

  31|                                SkillsUSA State President.

  33|                                days, and presented the students with an opportunity to develop leadership skills and perform community
  34|                                service at an area YMCA Day Camp.
  35|                          • The SkillsUSA local competition was held in January 2013. The competitions took place over a three-
  36|                                week period, and included leadership competitions as well as technical and skill based competitions. The
  37|                                event culminated in a Local Awards Ceremony, held at the school on January 23, 2013.

  39|                                Vocational Technical School.            There, a grand total of forty-seven (47) medals were captured by Monty
  40|                                Tech students - fourteen (14) gold, nineteen (19) silver, and fourteen (14) bronze medals. Two students
  41|                                 also qualified to run for the State Executive Council. Remarkably, students in four contests swept the
  42|                                contest, obtaining all three medals. These included 3D Animation Technology, Medical Assisting, Early
  43|                                 Childhood Education, and Digital Cinema Production. Russell Holbert (Graphic Communications)

   1|                                                                  Postgraduate and Continuing Studies
   2|                      The Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program, also known as Monty Tech Nites, continues to update and add that emphasize a commitment to excellence by offering affordable, quality, and enjoyable educational
   3|                      experiences. For the Fall of 2012, Monty Tech offered 106 courses "in-house" and another 400+ were offered online. Approximately 900 seats were sold for Fall 2012 courses. In comparison, there were 108 "in-house" courses and,
   4|                      again, over 400 on-line courses offered during the Spring 2013 semester. Approximately 800 seats were sold for Spring 2013 courses.
   5|                       In April 2013 the postgraduate program Technicians. The students took their practical successfully graduated our second class of fifteen Emergency Medical exam at Monty Tech and proceeded to take their written exam at a
   6|                       state-designated facility. Several graduates have already found employment in their chosen field.
   7|                       The Director of the Postgraduate & Continuing Studies Program actively seeks information to develop new (and expand existing) certificate and licensure programs that align with regional workforce needs and employment trends.
   8|                                                                          Practical Nursing Program
   9|                       The Practical Nursing Program is designed to prepare graduates to practice safely, ethically and in a caring manner for patients who are experiencing common variations in health status in diverse health care settings. This mission,
  10|                       which is consistent with the philosophy and goals of the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District, accomplished the following:
  11|                             • Identifies a strong relationship between academic and vocational preparation
  12|                             • Supports the maintenance of a positive and caring learning and practice environment productively in an ever-changing technical and diverse society
  13|                        In November 2012 the Practical Nursing Program hosted a site visit to obtain accreditation by the Council on
  14|                       Occupational Education (COE). We received full accreditation for six (6) years, which allows the program to participate in the U.S. Department of Education student loan program and provides the Monty Tech Evening Division with a
  15|                        national accreditation status.
  16|                        In Fall 2013 the Practical Nursing Program hosted a site visit and evaluation from the Dept. of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE). The Practical Nursing Program was approved as a Chapter 74 adult educational
  17|                        program. This will provide the program with another revenue source along with our student tuition income.
  18|                        In June 2013 we graduated twenty-nine (29) students to enter the nursing profession and the class achieved an
  19|                       initial NCLEX pass rate of 94%. Over 85% of the 2013 graduates are currently working in the North Central area as LPNs in various health care settings from long term care, sub-acute care, clinics and prison health care.
  20|                        State University continues to garner state and national attention. Program Director, Holly Lafrance, remains Our experience and forethought in the development of the LN to BSN Bridge program with Fitchburg and Worcester
  21|                        committed to the Nurses of the Future Initiative, working closely with the Board of Higher Education as a member of the
  22|                        the highly respected National League of Nursing (NLN) annual conference in Washington DC in Fall 2013. academic progression model team, while the Monty Tech Practical Nursing instructional team was asked to present at
  23|                        While the Montachusett Regional Vocational Technical School District educational community is certainly proud of
  24|                        the achievements of our talented students, faculty, and staff, we continue to have an eye toward the future, always committed to improving our vocational and academic programming, strengthening key partnerships, and maintaining
  25|                         facilities that contribute to student success and achievement.                          As we look ahead, there are a number of
  26|                         programs and initiatives that we expect will have a positive impact on our school and students for years to come.
  27|                         Biotechnology: With funds provided by a Massachusetts Life Sciences Center Grant, the renovation of two science
  28|                         supplies and equipment that enable educators to introduce exciting lab offerings and curriculum to eager students. Two courses, Introduction to Biotechnology (BTC101- 4 credits) and Basic Solution and Media (BTW102-
  29|                         3 credits) will be offered to high school students through a unique middle college model, whereby college level coursework is embedded during the traditional high school schedule. The courses will be co-taught by a Mount

   1|                        Wachusett Community College faculty member and a Monty Tech instructor. The MWCC faculty member will serve
   2|                        as a visiting instructor, co-teaching the classes with a highly qualified science instructor.                             Upon successful
   3|                        completion of the courses, students who earn a 'B' or better are eligible to receive seven (7) college credits.
   4|                        Expanded AP Offerings: In September 2012, Monty Tech launched its first Advanced Placement courses, grounded
   5|                       in the belief that because today's vocational                  programs are rigorous and complex, high school academic
   6|                        offerings should be as well. With 69 students enrolled in two courses, we are delighted to report approximately 40% of
   7|                       these eager students earned college credit as a result of end-of-course test results. Pleased with these results,
   8|                        District officials are excited to expand AP offerings in the coming years, introducing courses such as AP
   9|                       Statistics, AP Studio Art, and even AP Environmental Science.
  10|                      Renovation of the School's Library: For several years, Monty Tech has been without a library. Though the school's faculty
  11|                      and staff   have gone to great lengths to provide alternatives to students                          during   this time, providing research
  12|                      databases, ebooks and audio books, the district has committed to renovating the existing facility, with the hopes of
  13|                      transforming it into a student-friendly 21st Century library. This extensive project, which is nearing completion, will provide
  14|                      students with 2,000 new fiction titles, access to a collection of approximately 6,200 titles, quiet study space, extensive
  15|                     research capabilities, and wireless access. We look forward to the library's grand re-opening in December 2013,
  16|                      providing the Monty Tech educational community with a library that more closely reflects the advanced technology
  17|                     and training available to students at Monty Tech.
  18|                       coursework to develop a broad                understanding of the scope of the state's new evaluation system, and in
  19|                       September/October 2013, educators participated in training related to developing SMART Goals. We are delighted
  20|                       to report that an evaluation tool has been agreed upon, and that teachers and administrators continue to work
  21|                       collaboratively to implement the new evaluation tool, with intentions of improving educational delivery across the
  22|                       District.
  23|                     Summer Camps: The school's new Dean of Admissions has proposed a Summer Camp program that will bring added life to
  24|                     our halls during the month of July. Interested 6th - 8th grade students in our eighteen sending communities will have an
  25|                     opportunity to attend one of four innovative summer enrichment programs. Each camp has been designed to introduce
  26|                     students to vocational programming, familiarize them with our school, and will culminate in an exciting field trip! Additionally,
  27|                     thanks to the generosity of the Monty Tech Foundation, scholarships will be available to students who qualify for free/reduced
  28|                    lunches, and all costs of the field trip will be covered for every participant.

===PAGE 59=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                                                                                             Enrollment
   2|                           On October 1, 2011, student enrollment at Monty Tech included 1,435 students in grades nine through twelve, Students are
   3|                           represented from every community in the district: Ashburnham (58), Ashby (37), Athol (110), Barre (39), Fitchburg (376),
   4|                            Gardner (173), Harvard (4), Holden (60), Hubbardston (56), Lunenburg (2), Petersham (8), Phillipston (17), Princeton (14),
   5|                            Royalston (24), Sterling (58), Templeton (110), Westminster (70), and Winchendon (127).
   6|                           Throughout 2011-2012, Monty Tech offered a variety of opportunities for students, parents, and community members to
   7|                           learn about and visit the school. In October 2011, approximately 1,000 district eighth graders participated in the annual
   8|                           "Tour Day" event. Students toured our twenty vocational/technical areas and learned about the school's challenging
   9|                           academic offerings and exciting athletic and extracurricular programs. Career Awareness Night offered interested students
  10|                           the opportunity to return in the evening with their family members, to further explore the facilities and talk with staff
  11|                           members.
  12|                           Each year, the Dean of Admissions conducts school visits, student interviews, and accepts applications for admissions.
  13|                           2011-2012 proved to be an exceptionally busy year for him, as the school received a record 823 applications for admission.
  14|                           Of those, 773 were from students hoping to enter our incoming freshman class. The balance of the applications came from
  15|                           students hoping to enter the school as upperclassmen. Because there are only a limited number of students the school can
  16|                           accept each year, 380 freshmen and 24 upperclassmen were admitted.
  17|                           The Vocational Interest Program (V.I.P.) offers area seventh and eighth grade students the chance to visit Monty Tech after
  18|                           school, and participate in hands-on learning experiences across a variety of vocational/technical areas. The program
  19|                           continued to attract a large number of students during the 2011-2012 school year, serving approximately 675 area students.
  20|                                                                                       Class of 2012 Awards
  21|                           Members of the Class of 2012 were awarded approximately $50,000 in scholarships.                             The Monty Tech Foundation
  22|                           generously provided $21,000 in scholarships to graduating seniors, ranging in amounts of $100 to $1,500. The Foundation
  23|                           also awarded $6,000 to the practical nursing graduates. Once again, local and state organizations, as well as generous
  24|                           individuals, continue to recognize the ability and potential of Monty Tech graduates in the form of financial donations. The
  25|                           School Committee, administration, faculty, and graduates themselves, are grateful for this support.
  26|                           Articulation Agreements with local colleges also play an important role in helping reduce the cost of higher education.
  27|                           Qualified Monty Tech students are eligible to receive college credits through a number of articulation agreements with
  28|                           public and private colleges across the country. Just over 70% of the graduating class of 2012 reported plans to enroll at
  29|                           either a 2-year college, 4-year college/university, or a technical/trade school upon graduation. By earning college credits
  30|                           while still in high school, these students will benefit by saving both time and money as they pursue advanced educational
  31|                           programs.
  32|                                                                                          Financial Report
  33|                           Numerous challenges were faced during the development of the fiscal year 2011-2012 budget, including rising health
  34|                           insurance and student transportation costs. Considerable effort was put forth by the School Committee, administration, and
  35|                           staff to develop a cost-effective budget. The final fiscal year 2011-2012 Educational Plan totaled $22,744,779, which
  36|                           represents a $2.4% increase over the 2010-2011 Educational Plan.
  37|                           The District was audited in August 2012 as part of the yearly financial audit by the accounting firm of Melanson, Heath and Co. from Greenfield, MA and an "excellent" report is anticipated.
  38|                                                                                       Grants and Contracts
  39|                           Monty Tech continues to pursue grant funding on an annual basis. These funds assist in providing many educational and
  40|                           social services to the student population. For fiscal year 2012, state and federal grant sources provided the school with
  41|                                                                                               55

===PAGE 60=== (ocr, 7 lines)
   1|                           mproving Teacher Quality, Special Education Services and Program Improvement, Title | Support, Drug Free Schools 1,407,959. Programs funded by these grants include: Essential Health Services, Social Intervention and Mediation
   2|                           Perkins Occupational Education, and Summer Academic Support.
   3|                           In addition to state and federal grant sources, Monty Tech was also the recipient of private and competitive grant awards
   4|                          totaling $20,918. For Fiscal Year 2012, Monty Tech received $13,251 in grant funds from the Community Foundation of North Central Massachusetts, to support the Student Spaceflight Experiment Program. The Massachusetts Space Grant
   5|                           Consortium and Nypro also contributed to this ground-breaking program, by adding an additional $7,667 in grant funds.
   6|                           Grants totaled $1,428,877 for fiscal year 2012.

  25|                              ARTICLE 20. To see if the Town will vote to approve the establishment of an Agricultural Commission to represent,
  26|                             preserve and enhance farming in the community. The Town General Bylaws will be amended to include a new
  27|                              Article 29, entitled "Agricultural Commission," which will read as follows:
  28|                              Section 1. Said Commission, once appointed shall develop a work plan to guide its activities. Such activities may
  29|                              include, but are not limited to the following: encouraging the continued pursuit of agriculture in Lunenburg;
  30|                              promoting agricultural-based economic opportunities in Town; mediating, advocating, educating and/ or negotiating
  31|                              on farming issues; working for the preservation of prime agricultural lands; and pursuing all initiatives appropriate to
  32|                              retaining farming in Lunenburg.
  33|                              Section 2. The Commission shall consist of five members appointed by the Board of Selectmen at the
  34|                              recommendation of the Town Manager. A majority of the membership shall be substantially engaged in the pursuit of
  35|                              agriculture. Members shall be appointed to serve three year terms, except that the initial terms of office shall be
  36|                              staggered at the discretion of the Board of Selectmen, so that the approximately one-third of the terms shall expire
  37|                              each year. The appointing authority shall fill any vacancy based on the unexpired term being vacated at the
  38|                              recommendations of the standing Commission.
  39|                              Section 3. The Agricultural Commission will assist in maintaining agriculture in Lunenburg, Specifically, the
  40|                              Agricultural Commission will administer the Right-to-Farm bylaw, and will hold additional responsibilities as charged
  41|                                                                                                  84

===PAGE 89=== (ocr, 41 lines)
   1|                          by the Board of Selectmen. The Right-to-Farm bylaw and creation of an Agricultural Commission are components of
   2|                          the Commonwealth's "Sustainable Development" program, thereby helping Lunenburg become eligible for various
   3|                          state grant programs. [Submitted by Petition] Board of Selectmen recommends approval, 5 - 0. Finance Committee reports no
   4|                          direct financial impact. VOTED UNANIMOUSLY.
